5 
II. 
Purpose of Testimony and Overview of Filing 
6 
Q. 
What is the purpose of the Panel’s testimony? 
7 
A. 
The purpose of the Panel’s testimony is to support KEDNY’s request to 
8 
increase gas delivery rates.  In support of this request, the Panel will: 
9 
 
(i) 
present KEDNY’s historic and forecast data for various periods in a 
10 
manner consistent with the Commission’s regulations and policies; 
11 
 
(ii) 
describe the Company’s forecast of operation and maintenance 
12 
(“O&M”) expenses, as well as adjustments to those expenses that were made 
13 
to normalize the historic test year; 
14 
 
(iii) 
describe the Company’s efforts to manage its cost of service; 
15 
 
(iv) 
discuss the development of KEDNY’s gas rate base; 
16 
 
(v) 
support the Company’s forecast and amortization of certain regulatory 
17 
deferral balances; and  
18 
 
(vi) 
describe proposed treatment for deferrals that the Commission has 
19 
previously adopted, propose some changes to existing deferral mechanisms, 
20 
and propose new deferral mechanisms.  
21 
9


--- Page 12 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 7 of 136 
 
The Panel will also describe the Company’s efforts in connection with the 
1 
preparation of this rate filing to review the accuracy of costs in the historic test 
2 
year and to appropriately normalize those results for known and measurable 
3 
changes that will occur between the end of the historic test year and the rate 
4 
year. 
5 
 
6

members of the Panel and which, in all cases, refer to KEDNY:

Appendix A 
Description of Regulatory Assets and Liabilities 
11 
 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
What is the historic test year in this proceeding?   
14 
A. 
The historic test year in this proceeding is the twelve (12) months ended 
15 
September 30, 2015 (“Historic Test Year”).  The Historic Test Year data 
16 
consists of the costs recorded on the books of KEDNY, including: (i) costs 
17 
from within the Company; (ii) costs charged to KEDNY from National Grid 
18 
Service Company and National Grid Engineering & Survey, Inc. (collectively, 
19 
the “Service Company”); and (iii) costs charged to KEDNY from other 
20 
affiliated companies.   
21 
 
22 
Q. 
What Historic Test Year and Rate Year information is the Company 
23 
presenting? 
24 
A. 
The Company is presenting its operating results for the Historic Test Year and 
25 
forecast data for the rate year, which is the twelve (12) months ending 
26 
December 31, 2017 (“Rate Year”).  In addition, the Company is including 
27 
11


--- Page 14 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 9 of 136 
forecast data for the 12 months ending December 31, 2018 (“Data Year 1”) 
1 
and December 31, 2019 (“Data Year 2”) (collectively, the “Data Years”) for 
2 
information purposes and to facilitate the possibility of a multi-year rate 
3 
settlement in this proceeding.  The forecast data provide the basis for 
4 
computing the revenue requirement for the Rate Year and Data Years in this 
5 
proceeding.  The information presented in this filing is consistent with that 
6 
required under the Commission’s Statement of Policy on Test Periods in 
7 
Major Rate Proceedings. 
8 
 
9 
Q. 
What are KEDNY’s revenue requirements and associated revenue 
10 
deficiencies based on current rates for its gas business? 
11 
A. 
Based on the cost of service set forth in the Company’s filing, for the Rate 
12 
Year, KEDNY’s revenue deficiency is $244.953 million, based on a 9.94 
13 
percent return on equity and a 48 percent common equity ratio, as supported 
14 
by Company Witnesses Ann E. Bulkley and Stephen H. Caldwell.  If a three-
15 
year settlement is reached, the Company proposes a return on equity of 10.44 
16 
percent be used to calculate the revenue requirement, as supported by Ms. 
17 
Bulkley and Mr. Caldwell.   
18 
 
19 
Exhibit __ (RRP-1) shows Historic Test Year and Rate Year rates of return of 
20 
4.23 percent and of 2.39 percent, respectively, which equate to returns on 
21 
12


--- Page 15 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 10 of 136 
equity of 3.67 percent and 0.72 percent, respectively.  These returns assume 
1 
no deferral of property taxes in the Historic Test Year or Rate Year and no 
2 
deferral for Pension and OPEB expenses in the Rate Year.  
3 
 
4 
Q. 
How does KEDNY propose to address the current revenue deficiency? 
5 
A. 
The Company is proposing to reset base gas delivery rates, which were last set 
6 
in Case 06-G-1185 (the “2006 Rate Case”) and extended in Case 12-G-0544 
7 
(the “2012 Rate Plan Extension”).  The new base gas delivery rates proposed 
8 
here are intended to permit recovery of the cost of providing service and 
9 
provide KEDNY the opportunity to earn a fair return.     
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Does the Company illustrate an approach to mitigate the effect on 
12 
customers of the increase needed to address the Rate Year revenue 
13 
deficiency? 
14 
A.       Yes.  The bill impacts the Company is presenting are significant.  To ease the 
15 
impact on customers, the Company believes the revenue increase should be 
16 
phased in over multiple years through a settlement with Staff and interested 
17 
parties.  The table below illustrates how the necessary revenue increases could 
18 
be distributed over a three-year period..  
19

10 
III. 
Historic Test Year Cost Review, Trend Analysis, and LIPA Separation 
11 
Analysis 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
What steps did KEDNY undertake to ensure the accuracy of its Historic 
14 
Test Year costs?  
15 
A. 
While SAP was stabilized in September 2014, prior to the beginning of the 
16 
Historic Test Year, KEDNY recognizes the importance of demonstrating the 
17 
accuracy of its Historic Test Year costs, particularly in light of past data 
18 
challenges.  Accordingly, to validate the accuracy and integrity of Historic 
19 
Test Year costs and to facilitate Staff’s review of these costs, KEDNY 
20 
undertook extensive efforts to assure that costs incurred in the Historic Test 
21 
Year were thoroughly reviewed and appropriately normalized.  KEDNY 
22 
15


--- Page 18 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 13 of 136 
commissioned a public accounting firm, PricewaterhouseCoopers LLP 
1 
(“PwC”), to conduct an independent review of the accounting for costs in the 
2 
Historic Test Year.  PwC’s review supplemented the Company’s own 
3 
extensive internal review of the Historic Test Year data.  
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Please describe the scope of PwC’s review of Historic Test Year costs. 
6 
A. 
The Historic Test Year includes (i) costs charged to KEDNY (direct or 
7 
allocated) from National Grid Service Company and (ii) costs either 
8 
originating in KEDNY or charged to it by its affiliates.  PwC reviewed costs 
9 
in both categories to confirm that the Historic Test Year contains costs 
10 
appropriately charged to KEDNY.  The review was similar to the one 
11 
conducted by Ernst & Young in Niagara Mohawk’s last rate filing; however, 
12 
the scope of PwC’s review was expanded to include costs originating in the 
13 
operating companies and charges to capital projects to provide the Company 
14 
with additional assurance that Historic Test Year costs are correctly stated.   
15 
 
16 
 
Table 1 reflects the total National Grid Service Company and operating 
17 
company costs considered for examination and the total amounts PwC 
18 
examined, including a percentage breakdown.   
19 
 
20 
 
21 
16

3 
Q. 
Please describe the approach taken by PwC to conduct its review.  
4 
A. 
PwC’s review was conducted in accordance with attestation standards 
5 
established by the American Institute of Certified Public Accountants.  The 
6 
review was focused on testing that costs were charged appropriately to 
7 
KEDNY, including those allocated from National Grid Service Company, and 
8 
reflected the actual costs incurred to deliver service during the Historic Test 
9 
18

Year.  The review was designed to identify errors, positive or negative, that

3

4

5

7

8

9

12

15

Additional Reviews 
1 
Q. 
Did KEDNY conduct any additional review of Historic Test Year costs? 
2 
A. 
Yes, KEDNY performed several additional reviews of Historic Test Year 
3 
costs to provide further assurance that these costs were accurate and 
4 
appropriate to use as a basis for forecasting the Rate Year.  The first additional 
5 
review consisted of a review of vendor charges organized by vendor name to 
6 
identify potential charges that should be removed from the Historic Test Year.  
7 
This included, for example, charitable donations, lobbying expenses, and 
8 
other charges that should be accounted for below the line for ratemaking 
9 
purposes.  The second additional review consisted of a review of O&M 
10 
charges originating from the Service Company and from KEDNY and its 
11 
affiliates organized by project title.  The third review consisted of a review of 
12 
manual journal entries for one-time or out-of-period adjustments that should 
13 
be removed from the Historic Test Year.  The last review was a review of 
14 
employee expenses, including a keyword search to identify charges that 
15 
should be normalized.  If a keyword came up during the search, or if a charge 
16 
did not appear reasonable, the Company performed a more detailed review to 
17 
investigate whether the charge should be removed.  The adjustments from 
18 
these internal reviews are presented as separate line items in Exhibit ___ 
19 
(RRP-2), Schedule 1.

Trend Analysis 
1 
Q. 
Did the Company compare Historic Test Year O&M costs with prior 
2 
period costs to determine the major drivers of any changes?   
3 
A. 
Yes.  The Company performed an analysis to look at changes in expenses 
4 
incurred by KEDNY from calendar year 2005 through the Historic Test Year.  
5 
The purpose of the review was to look at trends in costs by functional area 
6 
incurred by KEDNY directly, as well as from the Service Company and 
7 
affiliates that charge KEDNY.  Over the past few years, there have been a 
8 
number of changes in the organizational structure of the service companies 
9 
and KEDNY such that it is not possible to analyze changes in the costs 
10 
charged to KEDNY from the service companies by examining only the overall 
11 
level of Service Company costs charged to KEDNY from year to year.  
12 
Because of these organizational changes, the Company’s costs must be 
13 
examined on a total basis to draw meaningful conclusions regarding year over 
14 
year variations.  This is accomplished by combining (i) costs incurred directly 
15 
by KEDNY, (ii) costs charged to KEDNY from the Service Company, and 
16 
(iii) costs charged to KEDNY from other affiliates.   
17 
 
18 
 
The analysis of costs based on the combination of direct and indirect charges 
19 
provides improved clarity concerning the factors driving cost increases and 
20 
decreases by removing the impact of organizational changes that merely result 
21 
28


--- Page 31 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 26 of 136 
in shifts rather than absolute changes in costs from year to year.  As National 
1 
Grid has moved to strengthen its Shared Services organization to realize the 
2 
benefits discussed below, costs have shifted from KEDNY direct costs to 
3 
costs that are allocated to KEDNY from the Service Company and its other 
4 
affiliates.  Examples of this cost shifting are costs incurred by the Shared 
5 
Services and Customer functions.  Exhibit __ (RRP-2), Schedule 3 presents a 
6 
comparison of total O&M costs for the period from calendar year 2005 
7 
through the Historic Test Year.   
8 
 
 
9 
Q. 
What does Exhibit __ (RRP-2), Schedule 3 show? 
10 
A. 
The analysis presented on Exhibit __ (RRP-2), Schedule 3 shows that over the 
11 
period 2005 - September 30, 2015, the total O&M costs incurred by KEDNY 
12 
increased from $334.857 million to $412.115 million, an increase of $77.258 
13 
million.  Significant contributors to the $77.258 million difference include 
14 
increased operational workload (e.g., leaks, damage prevention, 
15 
disconnects/reconnects, et cetera.) ($32.3 million), increased benefits costs 
16 
($15.9 million), increased energy efficiency program expenses ($19.9 
17 
million), greater SIR costs ($5.6 million), increased injuries and damages 
18 
($19.3 million), and the effect of more than ten years of inflation ($33.7 
19 
million).   Offsetting those increases, however, were significant decreases in 
20 
the Company’s uncollectible accounts costs (reduced $30.9 million) and 
21 
29


--- Page 32 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 27 of 136 
regulatory assessment costs (reduced $13.9 million).  Exhibit __ (RRP-2), 
1 
Schedule 3 also shows that between 2005 and the Historic Test Year, other 
2 
business initiatives and departmental measures reduced costs a further $12.6 
3 
million.   
4 
 
 
5 
 
Regarding the source of the cost changes from CY 2005 to the Historic Test 
6 
Year, Exhibit __ (RRP-2), Schedule 3 illustrates that the majority of increased 
7 
costs over the 2005 – Historic Test Year period is attributable to costs directly 
8 
incurred at KEDNY ($98.867 million).  Costs from other companies actually 
9 
decreased significantly (reduced $21.773 million), while Service Company 
10 
costs were essentially flat (increased $0.162 million). 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Does the trend analysis support the conclusion that the service company 
13 
structure is beneficial to KEDNY and its customers? 
14 
A. 
Yes.  As Exhibit __ (RRP-2), Schedule 3 shows, costs from the Service 
15 
Company to KEDNY have remained essentially flat for over ten years during 
16 
a period when other operating costs KEDNY has experienced have increased.  
17 
The service company structure positions the Company to (i) attain the benefits 
18 
of economies of scale and scope available from the provision of centralized 
19 
services to a number of operating entities in a manner that ensures that no 
20 
operating entity is cross-subsidizing another, (ii) improve service quality 
21 
30


--- Page 33 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 28 of 136 
throughout the National Grid organization as a result of the enhanced job 
1 
differentiation and specialization that results from the provision of services on 
2 
a centralized basis to a number of operating entities, (iii) improve reliability of 
3 
services provided within the organization, and (iv) implement enhanced 
4 
controls and uniformity of methods and practices throughout the National 
5 
Grid US business. 
6 
 
7 
Q. 
How does the Company determine that the costs it incurs from the 
8 
Service Company are reasonable? 
9 
A. 
The Service Company provides services to the US operating companies, 
10 
including KEDLI and KEDNY, pursuant to the terms of service agreements 
11 
that are filed with the Commission.  The service agreements set forth the 
12 
services provided and charges for those services (i.e., services are provided at 
13 
actual cost).  National Grid has created a process to provide visibility to the 
14 
Jurisdictional Presidents and their leadership teams of all costs charged to the 
15 
operating entities through the service companies.  This information provides 
16 
the jurisdictional leadership with regular opportunities to challenge and 
17 
question service company charges.   
18 
 
19 
 
Under National Grid’s jurisdictional model, the jurisdictional leadership teams 
20 
have the key role of assuring that customers are receiving appropriate 
21 
31


--- Page 34 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 29 of 136 
treatment from affiliates in terms of service quality and costs.  Service level 
1 
agreements (“SLAs”) have been established between the jurisdictions and the 
2 
National Grid Service Company functions to provide a management 
3 
framework.   
4 
 
5 
 
National Grid continues to refine, improve and expand the SLAs.  
6 
Specifically, in accordance with the Niagara Mohawk Joint Proposal adopted 
7 
in Cases 12-E-0201 and 12-G-0202 (“Niagara Mohawk Joint Proposal”), the 
8 
SLAs were enhanced to include detailed service descriptions that align with 
9 
the budgeted cost for each service, as well as metrics and targets to 
10 
demonstrate and ensure that the services provided by National Grid Service 
11 
Company achieve reasonable levels of quality and performance.  In 
12 
accordance with the recommendations in the Overland Consulting audit of 
13 
Service Company transactions (Case 10-M-0451), National Grid implemented 
14 
the enhanced SLAs for all of the New York operating companies, including 
15 
KEDNY.  National Grid also implemented a governance framework for the 
16 
SLAs that includes monthly meetings to discuss budget and/or metric 
17 
performance.  During these meetings, performance issues are addressed or 
18 
escalated for further review.   
19 
 
20 
32


--- Page 35 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 30 of 136 
 
National Grid further agreed, in the Niagara Mohawk Joint Proposal, to 
1 
develop external cost comparisons to demonstrate and ensure that SLA costs 
2 
are reasonable relative to appropriate market alternatives.  To date, National 
3 
Grid has benchmarked two-thirds of the National Grid Service Company 
4 
functions.  In almost all instances, the benchmarking has shown that the 
5 
services provided by the National Grid Service Company functions compare 
6 
favorably to third-party rates obtained in the market.  The enhanced SLAs and 
7 
external cost comparisons for the New York operating companies have been 
8 
submitted to Staff.   
9 
 
10 
 
In response to recommendations in the recent New York Gas Management 
11 
Audit (13-G-0009) (“Gas Management Audit”), National Grid is further 
12 
enhancing the SLAs (to become effective in FY17) to provide consequences 
13 
for failing to meet the performance metrics in the SLAs.

15 
LIPA Separation Costs 
16 
Q. 
Please provide background on the Long Island Power Authority’s 
17 
(“LIPA”) transition to a new manager for its Long Island electric system. 
18 
A.  
Since 1998, a National Grid affiliate, National Grid Electric Services LLC, 
19 
had managed LIPA’s Long Island electric transmission and distribution 
20 
(“T&D”) system pursuant to a long term Management Services Agreement 
21 
33


--- Page 36 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 31 of 136 
(“MSA”).  Under the MSA, National Grid provided a comprehensive set of 
1 
electric operations, customer and corporate support services for LIPA’s 
2 
electric distribution business.  In 2012, LIPA announced it would transition to 
3 
a new manager, Public Service Enterprise Group Long Island LLC (“PSEG-
4 
LI”) in January 2014.   
5 
 
6 
Q. 
How did the transition of the LIPA management contract impact 
7 
National Grid’s New York operating companies? 
8 
A. 
LIPA’s decision to select a new T&D manager necessitated the transfer of 
9 
more than 2,000 National Grid personnel, as well as various assets and 
10 
business systems primarily supporting electric operations, to PSEG-LI to 
11 
ensure that electric service on Long Island would not be disrupted and that the 
12 
electric business would continue to benefit from an experienced workforce.  
13 
Among the systems and departments that had to be separated were National 
14 
Grid’s customer, operations and other functions that had historically supported 
15 
both the gas and electric distribution businesses on Long Island.  This resulted 
16 
in the loss of certain operational synergies that had been realized by National 
17 
Grid’s combined electric and gas functions, forcing National Grid to consider 
18 
alternative solutions to cost-effectively meet the needs of customers of 
19 
KEDLI, National Grid’s other downstate New York gas distribution business.  
20 
34


--- Page 37 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 32 of 136 
In addition, the separation resulted in the loss of certain cost efficiencies that 
1 
affected all of National Grid’s regulated affiliates in the US.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please describe National Grid’s approach to the transition. 
4 
A. 
National Grid developed a comprehensive transition plan that focused on 
5 
enhancing service quality levels while also mitigating the cost impacts to 
6 
customers.  For example, to provide customer service support to KEDLI 
7 
following the transition, National Grid looked to leverage potential synergies 
8 
with KEDNY.   
9 
 
10 
Q.  
What were the major transition initiatives? 
11 
A. 
Separation of the customer service organization that had historically served 
12 
both LIPA and KEDLI provided the biggest challenge.  This challenge was 
13 
met by several major customer initiatives, including (i)  replacing the legacy 
14 
Long Island Lighting Company customer information system, which, while 
15 
KEDLI experienced billing accuracy issues in the transition, led to improved 
16 
billing functionality for KEDLI customers; (ii) retrofitting 568,000 gas meters 
17 
with automated reading devices, which led to improved meter reading 
18 
accuracy, better post storm restoration and higher overall customer 
19 
satisfaction; and (iii) relocating the Long Island customer call center to an 
20 
existing National Grid customer call center in Brooklyn, New York.  These 
21 
35


--- Page 38 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 33 of 136 
and other post-transition initiatives established a new customer platform for 
1 
KEDLI.  On the operations side, National Grid’s transition plans included the 
2 
relocation of its Gas Control Center to a dedicated gas facility in Melville and 
3 
consolidation of downstate warehouse operations in an existing facility in 
4 
Greenpoint, New York. 
5 
 
6 
Q. 
How did National Grid support LIPA’s transition to PSEG-LI? 
7 
A. 
National Grid dedicated significant resources to assist PSEG-LI’s execution of 
8 
its transition plans for the Long Island electric distribution business.  First, as 
9 
noted above, National Grid and PSEG-LI ensured their respective businesses 
10 
were appropriately staffed with qualified personnel by transferring National 
11 
Grid employees to PSEG-LI.  National Grid also supported PSEG-LI’s efforts 
12 
to transition more than 700 business systems and associated data.  Finally, 
13 
National Grid committed to continue to support storm restoration efforts on 
14 
Long Island by making certain gas field personnel not engaged in gas 
15 
restoration work available to assist PSEG-LI’s storm response.  As a result of 
16 
these efforts, on January 1, 2014, responsibility for the Long Island T&D 
17 
system and its 1.2 million customers transitioned smoothly to PSEG-LI.

17

3

4

Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel

Q. 
Please explain the reallocation of costs to the National Grid operating 
1 
companies resulting from the LIPA transition. 
2 
A. 
The transfer of the MSA resulted in certain “dis-synergies,” as certain costs 
3 
that were once shared between LIPA and the National Grid operating 
4 
companies are now allocated among the National Grid operating companies 
5 
only.  As reflected in Exhibit __ (RRP-2 ), Schedule 4, the increase in 
6 
allocated costs to National Grid’s operating companies is largely attributable 
7 
to a reallocation of costs for shared services, human resources, information 
8 
systems and procurement support that could not be mitigated following the 
9 
LIPA separation.   
10 
 
11 
Q. 
How did National Grid mitigate the cost impacts of the LIPA transition? 
12 
A. 
National Grid implemented cost effective solutions and leveraged synergies 
13 
with KEDNY and its other US operating companies.  For example, prior to 
14 
the LIPA transition, both gas and electric meters were read by National Grid 
15 
meter reading personnel.  Rather than simply replacing the meter reading 
16 
workforce that transferred to PSEG-LI, KEDLI evaluated the cost and benefits 
17 
of various meter reading options and determined that an automated meter 
18 
reading (“AMR”) program was the most cost effective, long term solution to 
19 
reading gas meters on Long Island.  Consolidating the downstate call center 
20 
function in MetroTech is an example where National Grid mitigated potential 
21 
39


--- Page 42 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 37 of 136 
cost increases by leveraging existing facilities.  Similarly, National Grid 
1 
undertook a cost-benefit approach in designing the customer office, customer 
2 
system and inventory management solutions.   
3 
 
4 
 
With regard to Service Company charges that had been allocated to LIPA 
5 
prior to the transition, these charges were reduced to some extent as a result of 
6 
the transition.  To support LIPA’s electric business post-transition, National 
7 
Grid employees, facilities and systems were either transferred or leased to 
8 
PSEG-LI, which had no presence on Long Island prior to the transition.  This 
9 
resulted in a reduction in National Grid’s Service Company costs.  While the 
10 
Service Company costs allocated to LIPA could not be totally eliminated, they 
11 
were reduced as much as possible. 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
How are any effects from the LIPA transition reflected in this case? 
14 
A. 
To the extent the LIPA transition had recurring cost impacts in the Historic 
15 
Test Year, such impacts were reflected in Historic Test Year costs and carried 
16 
forward to the Rate Year. 
17

IV. 
O&M Expenses 
7 
Q. 
Please explain the methodology for developing the forecast of O&M 
8 
expenses in the Rate Year and Data Years. 
9 
A. 
To develop the O&M forecasts, the Company began by determining the costs 
10 
incurred in the Historic Test Year for the various expense types.  Next, the 
11 
Company reviewed Historic Test Year charges to determine whether specific 
12 
costs should be normalized, for example, as out-of-period, misallocated, or 
13 
onetime charges.  The inflation factors set forth in Exhibit ___ (RRP-8) were 
14 
then applied to the majority of expense types to forecast the Rate Year and 
15 
Data Years.  Forecasts of certain expenses were developed using a more 
16 
comprehensive methodology than simply adjusting for inflation.  For 
17 
example, labor expense was developed by annualizing the monthly and 
18 
weekly employees on payroll as of the end of the Historic Test Year, adjusting 
19 
for known changes to the employee headcount, and applying contractual and 
20 
projected wage increases to forecast the Rate Year and Data Years.  
21 
41


--- Page 44 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 39 of 136 
Additionally, the Company adjusted forecast O&M expenses to reflect, for 
1 
example, incremental work projected for the Rate Year or costs associated 
2 
with new initiatives.   
3 
 
4 
Q. 
What assumptions did the Company make regarding non-labor inflation? 
5 
A. 
Except where specifically identified otherwise, the Company applied the non-
6 
labor inflation factor of 4.2964 percent to all non-labor expenses in the 
7 
Historic Test Year to forecast the Rate Year.  This factor is equivalent to a 
8 
1.8872 percent average annual increase between the Historic Test Year and 
9 
the Rate Year.  The Company applied the non-labor inflation factor of 2.1000 
10 
percent to forecast Data Year 1 and Data Year 2.  These factors represent the 
11 
forecast change in the GDP Chained Price Index obtained from the Blue Chip 
12 
Economic Indicators, as provided in Exhibit ___ (RRP-8).  The Panel 
13 
addresses calculation of the non-labor inflation factor later in its testimony.   
14 
 
15 
Q. 
What is the basis for the Rate Year and Data Years allocations between 
16 
expense and capital?   
17 
A. 
Except as otherwise indicated, costs in the Rate Year and Data Years are 
18 
allocated between expense and capital consistent with their Historic Test Year 
19 
allocation.

Q.

A.

14

15

16 
company is directly charged for that service.  If the service is for the benefit of 
17 
multiple companies, however, the charge is allocated to those companies 
18 
using an appropriate allocator.  Pages 1 through 3 of each schedule detail 
19 
charges to KEDNY from the Provider Companies, including the Service 
20

Q. 
Please describe how the Service Company charged KEDNY for services 
5 
during the Historic Test Year. 
6 
A. 
Service Company charges were either directly charged to KEDNY or to 
7 
another National Grid affiliate (where a service was performed for the benefit 
8 
of a single company) or, when a service was performed for multiple affiliates, 
9 
charges were aggregated and allocated to all affiliates that benefit from the 
10 
service using approved allocation methodologies.  These allocators have been 
11 
developed to accommodate the different types of costs that are incurred by 
12 
National Grid Service Company.   
 
13 
 
14 
The allocators are constructed based on cost causation principles and are 
15 
designed to maximize use of direct allocation codes where charges have been 
16 
incurred for the direct benefit of a single company.  When it is not appropriate 
17 
to use a direct allocation code, a cost causative code is assigned based on the 
18 
appropriate causation driver.  Where it is not appropriate to use a direct or cost 
19 
causative code, then a general allocator is used.  The general allocator used by 
20 
46


--- Page 49 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 44 of 136 
the Service Company is based on a three-point model using Net Margin, Net 
1 
Plant and Net O&M expense.

Q. 
Please describe the GTIS and KEDNY’s proposed treatment of project 
7 
costs in this case.  
8 
A. 
Between August 2011 and January 2013, KEDNY participated in multiple 
9 
collaborative sessions with Staff and energy services companies (“ESCOs”) 
10 
on ways to enhance the Company’s retail access program.  In an Order dated 
11 
October 23, 2014 in Case 14-G-0331, the Commission directed the Company 
12 
to implement the recommendations from the collaborative sessions, which 
13 
included a plan to deploy a new GTIS to support the Company’s retail access 
14 
program.  The Commission’s Order stated National Grid should make every 
15 
effort to implement the GTIS by April 1, 2015.   
16 
 
17 
 
National Grid identified a timetable to complete the work necessary for an 
18 
April 2015 implementation.  However, implementation of the GTIS proved 
19 
challenging because of the breadth and complexity of the system 
20 
53


--- Page 56 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 51 of 136 
(encompassing more than 240 discrete programs), evolving program 
1 
requirements, a higher than expected error rate during system testing, and 
2 
National Grid’s desire to establish a reliable and user-friendly platform 
3 
before deployment.  On March 20, 2015, National Grid received an 
4 
extension until November 1, 2015 to implement the GTIS.  However, delays 
5 
in testing to ensure the system was fully functioning and concerns about 
6 
deploying a new gas transportation system at the start of the winter heating 
7 
season resulted in the need for additional time to implement GTIS.  On 
8 
September 23, 2015, a further extension to April 30, 2016 was granted.   
9 
 
10 
National Grid has invested substantial amounts and dedicated numerous IS 
11 
resources to design, develop, and test the GTIS.  As shown in Exhibit ___ 
12 
(RRP-11), Workpapers to Exhibit __ (RRP-3), Schedule 9, Workpapers 2, 4

and 6, the IS capital forecast includes $10.150 million for the GTIS (shown

on the exhibit as Customer Choice ESCO Gas).  The rent expense revenue 
15

17 
A. 
In 2012, National Grid implemented a new IS delivery model that utilized 
18 
external partners, each of which performed a specific IS function.  The 
19 
Service Management Integrator (“SMI”) function, filled by Hewlett Packard 
20 
(“HP”), was designed to coordinate the activities of all the partners to ensure 
21 
55


--- Page 58 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 53 of 136 
they worked collaboratively for effective end-to-end delivery of high quality 
1 
services.  In addition, the SMI function managed the Service Desk, also 
2 
known as the Help Desk.  The Service Desk assists employees experiencing 
3 
problems with technological devices (laptops, desktops, mobile telephones, et 
4 
cetera), having difficulty accessing the National Grid network, or needing 
5 
assistance in performing a specific computing function.  The SMI Conversion 
6 
Mobilization Project replaces HP and brings both the SMI function and 
7 
management of the Service Desk in-house.  Although National Grid will be 
8 
in-sourcing the management of the Service Desk, there are no plans at this 
9 
time to in-source the Service Desk support staff who respond directly to 
10 
employee requests for assistance. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Why did National Grid decide to modify its approach to the SMI function 
13 
and management of the Service Desk? 
14 
A.   
Overall, National Grid has been able to operate the multi-vendor environment 
15 
successfully, leveraging the benefits of market-based pricing, access to new 
16 
skills, services, and capabilities, and flexibility to scale IS support to match 
17 
business demand.  As an external vendor, however, HP was challenged to 
18 
influence the other vendors and facilitate their collaboration.  In addition, as 
19 
the marketplace changed in an increasing “cloud” environment, National Grid 
20 
56


--- Page 59 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 54 of 136 
engaged additional suppliers.  National Grid decided that internal end-to-end 
1 
control of IS services would better support National Grid’s gas and electric 
2 
businesses. 
3 
 
4 
 
With respect to the Service Desk, beginning in late 2012 and continuing 
5 
through early 2014, internal satisfaction surveys showed that employees 
6 
utilizing the Service Desk were not satisfied with HP’s performance.  National 
7 
Grid worked with HP on process improvements and, by early 2014, was able 
8 
to bring the service to acceptable levels as reflected by internal satisfaction 
9 
surveys for that period.  Ultimately, however, National Grid decided that 
10 
internal management of the function is necessary to optimize the function and 
11 
achieve better employee user satisfaction.   
12 
 
13 
Closer management of the functions by National Grid personnel will facilitate 
14 
a higher level of performance both in isolated functions and in managing the 
15 
complex multi-vendor environment.  The changes to the SMI function and the 
16 
Service Desk are intended to help reduce business disruption as a result of IS 
17 
issues, and enable the optimization of the multi-vendor operating model 
18 
through consistent internal management.     
19 
 
20 
57

Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel

Q. 
Please describe the process used to convert full time and part time 
4 
employees into FTEs. 
5 
A. 
Full time equivalent status, shown on Page 13 of Schedule 23, was computed 
6 
by the following method: 
7 
 
(i) 
All full time employees were considered FTEs. 
8 
 
(ii) 
Part time employees were converted to FTEs using the following 
9 
formula: Average Part Time Employee Salary divided by Average Full Time 
10 
Employee Salary times Part Time Employee Count.  For KEDNY and Service 
11 
Company there were 0 and 36 part time management employees, respectively, 
12 
which equated to 0 and 19 FTEs.  The same procedure was used for 
13 
represented employees.  KEDNY and Service Company had 0 and 24 part 
14 
time employees included in the represented labor base, which equated to 0 and 
15 
19 FTEs, respectively.

Q.

5 
Year and Data Years? 
6

7 
effective wage increase (based on the current collective bargaining agreement) 
8 
annually for calendar 2016 through Data Year 2 (calendar 2019).

11 
the Rate Year and Data Years? 
12

13 
variable compensation, which provides direct incentives for such employees 
14 
to meet or exceed metrics tied to safe, reliable, and efficient performance, and 
15 
is included in the current collective bargaining agreement with the union.  The 
16 
forecast for the Rate Year and Data Years was calculated by applying the 
17 
target payout rate of 2.5 percent to average base wages adjusted for wage 
18 
increases, double time, and shift premiums and overtime.

Q. 
What percentage of labor costs did the Company forecast would be 
1 
capitalized in the Rate Year? 
2

3

4 
of labor costs capitalized.   
5 
 
6 
Q. 
Please describe 53rd week labor costs. 
7 
A. 
Every five years there is an additional, or 53rd, pay week.  Therefore, an 
8 
amount equal to one-fifth of the weekly cost (i.e., one day) is added to the 
9 
labor cost to reflect the normalized cost of the 53rd week.  Fifty-third week 
10 
labor costs are associated with any employee who is paid weekly.  Typically, 
11 
these are represented employees.  The 53rd week labor costs are added to the 
12 
total Rate Year represented labor costs found on Pages 6 and 7 of Schedule 
13 
23.   
14 
 
15 
Q. 
How were 53rd week labor costs calculated? 
16 
A. 
53rd week labor costs were calculated by taking the estimated annual Rate 
17 
Year labor costs for weekly paid employees divided by 2,080 hours and 
18 
multiplied by eight hours.  The 2,080 hours represents the total number of 
19 
hours worked in a normal calendar year and the eight hours represents the 
20 
normal work hours in a day (i.e., one-fifth of the week).  The calculation was 
21 
77


--- Page 80 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 75 of 136 
performed by salary band for full time represented employees and part time 
1 
represented employees.   
2

Schedule 24 – Transportation  
4 
 
Schedule 24 consists of thirteen pages and shows transportation costs incurred 
5 
by the Company.  The first four pages are the same as all other schedules, 
6 
with Page 4 detailing adjustments to normalize the Historic Test Year and 
7

9 
costs, such as taxes, lease expense, fuel costs, and other expenses. 
10 
 
11 
Q. 
What are the major cost drivers of transportation expense? 
12 
A. 
The majority of costs associated with transportation expense pertain to (1) 
13 
lease expense, (2) motor fuel, and (3) other expenses (e.g., vehicle parts and 
14 
maintenance costs).  Pages 5 through 7 detail the cost components in the 
15 
Historic Test Year, Rate Year, and Data Years.  Pages 9 through 13 provide a 
16 
further breakdown of the components in developing the forecast.

18 
Transportation Lease Expense 
19 
Q. 
Please describe the components of transportation lease expense. 
20 
78


--- Page 81 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 76 of 136 
A. 
Transportation lease expense consists of vehicles on lease in the Historic Test 
1 
Year that will remain on lease through the Rate Year, and vehicles on lease in 
2 
the Historic Test Year that will be replaced before or during the Rate Year.  In 
3 
addition, the Company’s forecast of transportation lease expense reflects the 
4 
costs of additional vehicle leases needed to achieve the proposed incremental 
5 
work plan presented in this case.   
6 
 
7 
Q. 
How did the Company develop the forecast for transportation lease 
8 
expense? 
9 
A. 
The Company began with the vehicle inventory as of September 30, 2015.  
10 
From that, the Company first forecast the lease expense for existing leases as 
11 
of September 30, 2015 through the term of the leases.  Second, the Company 
12 
added the forecast of lease expense associated with replacement vehicles 
13 
scheduled to be acquired prior to or in the Rate Year and Data Years.  In 
14 
general, vehicles become eligible for replacement when fully amortized.  
15 
Third, the Company increased the number of vehicles in fleet inventory to 
16 
support the level of work the Company plans to deliver in the Rate Year.  
17 
Finally, the Company reduced the forecast by estimated gains to be received 
18 
from sales of replaced vehicles at auction.   
19 
 
20 
Q. 
Please explain how transportation lease expense is calculated. 
21 
79


--- Page 82 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 77 of 136 
A. 
Estimated delivery dates are established for the replacement and additional 
1 
vehicles along with projected acquisition costs.  Lease expenses are then 
2 
calculated using the acquisition cost, term of the lease, and projected interest 
3 
rate provided by the leasing company.   
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Why did the Company not simply adjust the Historic Test Year for 
6 
inflation to forecast the Rate Year and Data Years? 
7 
A. 
The Company does not believe that adjusting for inflation provides an 
8 
accurate projection of activity in the Rate Year and Data Years.  
9 
Transportation lease expense is impacted by changes in the Company’s fleet.  
10 
Specifically, increases or decreases in lease expense result from vehicles 
11 
being removed, replaced, or added to the Company’s fleet.  In addition, the 
12 
Company’s fleet is comprised of many different types of vehicles and 
13 
equipment.  These vehicles vary in costs and lease terms.  The Company’s 
14 
forecast takes into account these known and measurable changes and, 
15 
therefore, provides a more accurate and reasonable forecast than simply 
16 
adjusting for inflation.  
17 
 
18 
Transportation Motor Fuel Expense 
19

How did the Company develop the forecast for motor fuel expense?

A.

8 
procured parts.  Vehicle maintenance costs consist of actual costs paid to 
9 
vendors when work is performed on Company vehicles by outside vendors.

13 
forecast for the Rate Year and Data Years.

83


--- Page 86 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 81 of 136 
A. 
KEDNY has included adjustments to headcount for the Rate Year, as well as 
1 
the Data Years, to reflect incremental positions the Company has added or 
2 
will be adding since the end of the Historic Test Year.  These adjustments 
3

4

5

6 
 
7 
 
In general, to calculate the cost to KEDNY of each incremental position, the 
8 
Company first determined the average salary for the position band level 
9 
(exempt positions) or job classification (represented positions).  The Company 
10 
then applied a benefits loading factor to arrive at a fully loaded cost for the 
11 
respective position.  Thereafter, the Company applied a capital/operating 
12 
expense split for a position based on expected work complement.  Finally, the 
13 
Company applied the relevant company allocation to the operating expense 
14 
portion of the fully loaded position costs to arrive at the amount of operating 
15 
expense to be borne by KEDNY for the position.  The incremental position 
16 
costs were also adjusted by the relevant one percent productivity factor, which 
17 
is discussed later in this testimony.

the surcharge would be limited to a 2.5 percent increase from 
5 
KEDNY’s prior year’s aggregate revenues.  Any amount not recovered 
6 
in the prior year’s surcharge because that amount would have 
7 
increased KEDNY’s aggregate revenues by more than 2.5 percent will 
8 
be included in the following year’s surcharge.   
9 
Such a mechanism would allow KEDNY to continue recovering a portion of 
10 
the past SIR deferral balance, address the uncertainty of the timing and level 
11 
of SIR costs, and reduce the deferral balance for future customers.    
12 
An illustrative example of the Company’s recovery proposal is shown in 
13 
Exhibit ___ (RRP-9), Schedule 5.

Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 95 of 136 
V. 
Efficiency and Productivity Cost Reductions 
1 
Q. 
Please describe the PEX organization. 
2 
A. 
National Grid established the PEX organization to improve overall 
3 
performance, mitigate costs and enhance service to customers by simplifying 
4 
and improving end-to-end processes across the business.  This effort includes 
5 
improvements in everyday tasks such as establishing performance hubs that 
6 
provide opportunities for dialogue within and between business functions to 
7 
improve coordination and to address obstacles to performance, or identifying 
8 
a process improvement that could reduce costs or deliver benefits to 
9 
customers.  To accomplish these goals, PEX teams work with the business to 
10 
identify issues impacting performance and develop initiatives to attempt to 
11 
resolve these issues.  Once a potential solution has been identified, the 
12 
business function implements the initiative and the PEX teams create metrics 
13 
to track performance.  The recent gas management audit report commented 
14 
positively on National Grid’s process improvement efforts.   
15 
 
16 
Q. 
Please explain how the PEX initiatives are classified.  
17 
A. 
The initiatives are classified into three types based on the potential benefits 
18 
they could provide: 
19 
(i) 
Type I – the benefit has a direct, quantifiable and sustainable impact in 
20 
reducing costs.  The benefit could also add revenue.   
21 
98


--- Page 101 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 96 of 136 
(ii) 
Type II – the benefit is a process improvement that consists of 
1 
resources freed up or future cost or potential penalty avoidance. 
2 
(iii) 
Type III – the benefit consists of improvements to operational 
3 
performance such as customer service, reliability, safety or regulatory 
4 
processes that are not associated with cost savings or revenue 
5 
increases.   
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Please provide an example of a Type I initiative. 
8 
A. 
An example of a Type I initiative is the Improve Crew Start Time/Wrench 
9 
Time Customer Meter Services (“CMS”) initiative.  This initiative is aimed at 
10 
increasing the productive time for CMS field workers and thereby reducing 
11 
overtime costs.  The initiative seeks to implement process improvements to 
12 
create standard work that could be performed by lower skilled workers (e.g., 
13 
refueling and resupplying vehicles).  This would provide CMS field workers 
14 
with more wrench time, thereby increasing the number of jobs that can be 
15 
completed in a day on straight-time, without impacting the safety of customers 
16 
or employees.   
17 
 
18 
Q. 
Please provide an example of a Type II initiative. 
19 
A. 
The Home Energy Fair Practices Act (“HEFPA”) Notification Process 
20 
Improvement initiative is a Type II initiative designed to enhance public 
21 
99


--- Page 102 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 97 of 136 
safety and regulatory compliance.  In 2014, a records audit by Staff noted 
1 
multiple violations of the Commission’s regulation requiring that National 
2 
Grid’s internal HEFPA liaison be notified when a customer’s equipment is 
3 
disconnected for safety reasons.  The initiative developed a consistent 
4 
approach to capturing the necessary data and issuing notifications to ensure 
5 
compliance with the regulation and avoid future noncompliance penalties.  
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Please provide an example of a Type III initiative. 
8 
A. 
KEDNY Lead intake is the initial point of contact between a customer who is 
9 
interested in adding a gas service and the Company.  Questions from existing 
10 
customers are handled by representatives in other departments.  The Lead 
11 
Intake-Misdirected Calls initiative was implemented to address an increase in 
12 
the number of calls unrelated to new service requests, which resulted in 
13 
customer dissatisfaction when calls had to be transferred.  PEX teams 
14 
analyzed misdirected calls volumes and call types to understand the reason 
15 
behind the increase.  The teams worked with the business to update phone 
16 
numbers on websites and printed documents and revise IVR options to make 
17 
them more customer-friendly, thereby improving the accuracy of option 
18 
selection and enhancing customer satisfaction. 
19 
 
20 
Q. 
How are the PEX initiatives and benefit targets established? 
21 
100


--- Page 103 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 98 of 136 
A. 
PEX teams brainstorm ideas to identify potential initiatives to be evaluated as 
1 
opportunities to deliver process improvements.  Initiatives may be specific to 
2 
an individual operating company or designed to benefit multiple companies. 
3 
 
4 
As a way of prioritizing initiatives, the PEX teams assign a rough estimate of 
5 
the potential benefit as an initial proxy until better information is developed as 
6 
to the benefit that can be achieved.  The benefits initially assigned to 
7 
initiatives are aspirational, and intended to motivate the PEX teams and the 
8 
business functions to optimize the savings that could potentially be achieved.  
9 
In some cases, the initial estimate is the function’s budget gap that the teams 
10 
hope to close.  Estimates are included for initiatives even if they are not 
11 
expected to deliver savings.  For example, for Type II penalty avoidance 
12 
initiatives, the projected benefit is the potential negative revenue adjustment. 
13 
 
14 
Benefit estimates are dynamic.  As work on the initiatives progresses, the 
15 
estimates are refined and updated as the PEX teams capture more information 
16 
and insight on the potential benefits.  Because the initial estimates are 
17 
aspirational, they often differ from the savings that are actually achievable.  
18 
Estimates change as the initiatives move from concept to maturity and new 
19 
information is developed.  Initiatives that prove to be not viable are closed or 
20 
placed on hold.   
21 
101


--- Page 104 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 99 of 136 
Q. 
What is the current status of the PEX initiatives? 
1 
A. 
For fiscal year (“FY”) 16 (April 1, 2015 – March 31, 2016), the PEX teams 
2 
identified the 35 most impactful initiatives (the “Top 35”) on which to focus 
3 
their resources and attention.  These initiatives were prioritized because they 
4 
(i) had the greatest potential to bring operating costs more in line with 
5 
revenues without compromising the ability to provide safe and reliable 
6 
service, or (ii) had the greatest potential to improve a process or enhance 
7 
service to customers even if no tangible savings would result.  The initial 
8 
aspirational estimate for the Top 35 was $30 million plus in Type I O&M 
9 
savings across the US. 
10 
As work on the Top 35 progressed, the list of initiatives was updated and the 
11 
aspirational savings estimate was revised to $13.78 million in Type I O&M 
12 
savings across the US.  While initiatives outside the Top 35 continue, 
13 
resources have been focused on the Top 35 first, allowing the business and 
14 
PEX teams to deliver the Top 35 in the most optimal manner.   
15 
 
16

1

4

5 
 
6 
Q. 
Please describe the method used to calculate the provision for FIT and 
7 
SIT in the Rate Year and Data Years. 
8 
A. 
Beginning with operating income before income taxes, the Company made 
9 
adjustments for those items that are treated differently for book and income 
10 
tax purposes and that have a net effective tax rate impact.  For example, only 
11 
50 percent of meals and entertainment expenses are allowed as a tax 
12 
deduction.  Specifically, Pages 1 through 3 of Exhibit ___ (RRP-6) detail the 
13 
FIT and SIT calculation beginning with net income before tax multiplied by 
14 
the statutory federal or state tax rate presently effective for the Rate Year.  
15 
Tax additions and deductions that are permanent in nature or flow through to 
16 
customers are separately listed to arrive at net current federal and state tax 
17 
expense.  The federal portion includes the benefit of the state tax deduction.   
18 
 
19

2 
The estimated state tax effect of the rate change is $6.577 million and the 
3 
Company is proposing to include the effect of the rate change over ten years.  
4 
This amortization impacts the Company’s effective tax rate.

for the same period were subtracted.  Plant retirements were developed by

Q. 
Does the Company’s forecast of net utility plant reflect any changes in 
1 
capitalization policy? 
2 
A. 
Yes.  As described in the testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations 
3 
Panel, the Company is proposing changes to its capitalization policy for 
4 
corrosion control activities.  Some corrosion control tools and activities 
5 
currently are expensed; however, applicable accounting principles and 
6 
regulations permit the capitalization of certain of those items.  The Company 
7 
is proposing to capitalize certain corrosion control activities in accordance 
8 
with Accounting Standards Codification 360, FERC’s accounting regulations 
9 
and International Accounting Standard 16.  This change will also standardize 
10 
the accounting treatment of these work items across National Grid’s 
11 
downstate New York gas distribution companies.  Testing and inspection 
12 
activities related to corrosion control will remain as expensed items.     
13 
 
14 
Q. 
What is the impact to the revenue requirement of this change? 
15 
A. 
In the Historic Test Year, this change resulted in shift from pre-tax expense to 
16 
capital expenditure of $0.698 million.  Adjusting for inflation produces a shift 
17 
of $0.728 million from pre-tax expense to capital expenditure in the Rate 
18 
Year, which is embedded in the gas capital forecast shown in Exhibit ___ 
19 
(RRP-7), Schedule 1, Page 4.  The effect on operating expense is included in 
20

the revenue requirement in Exhibit ___ (RRP-3), Schedule 27, Page 4.  No 
1

B. 
Deferred Taxes 
4 
Q. 
Please explain Schedule 3 of Exhibit ___ (RRP-7). 
5 
A. 
Schedule 3 shows the average federal and state ADIT for the Rate Year and 
6 
Data Years, which reduce rate base. 
7 
 
8 
Q. 
How did the Company develop the forecast of ADIT? 
9 
A. 
The Company began with the actual ADIT balances as of September 2015 and 
10 
calculated the forecast based on plant movements and changes in regulatory 
11 
assets and liabilities.  The Company then made onetime adjustments to arrive 
12 
at the forecast for the Rate Year and Data Years.   
13 
 
14 
Q. 
Please explain the change in ADIT related to plant movements. 
15 
A. 
All movements in ADIT related to plant are a direct result of forecast plant 
16 
additions.  The Company forecast tax deductions related to plant additions in 
17 
three layers.  The first layer was new plant additions eligible for the repair 
18 
deduction.  The repair rate was applied as a percentage of total capital 
19 
expenditures.  The percentage was an estimate based on a trend analysis of the 
20 
115


--- Page 118 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 113 of 136 
actual results of the prior six fiscal years’ tax return study for gas plant 
1 
repairs.   
2 
 
3 
 
The second layer was the new plant additions, after the deduction for repairs, 
4 
eligible for bonus depreciation.  The bonus rates used were based on new 
5 
legislation passed December 2015 in the “Protecting Americans from Tax 
6 
Hikes Act of 2015” (“PATH Act”).  The Company deducts 50 percent for 
7 
assets forecast to be placed in-service for calendar year 2015 - 2017.  The 
8 
deduction is reduced to 40 percent for calendar year 2018 and 30 percent for 
9 
calendar year 2019.  Bonus depreciation is only available for federal tax 
10 
purposes because New York has decoupled from the bonus depreciation 
11 
provisions.   
12 
 
The remaining tax deduction is based on new plant additions, after the 
13 
deductions for repairs and bonus depreciation, subject to accelerated 
14 
depreciation deductions under a 20-year Modified Accelerated Cost Recovery 
15 
System (“MACRS”) life.  The Company also deducts cost of removal in the 
16 
year the cash is spent.  All tax plant deductions were compared to book 
17 
depreciation expense and a deferred tax liability was calculated on the 
18 
difference.   
19 
 
20 
116


--- Page 119 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 114 of 136 
Q. 
How did the Company reflect changes in regulatory assets and liabilities 
1 
in the forecast? 
2 
A. 
The movement in most regulatory assets and liabilities results in a book-tax 
3 
timing difference.  The existing deferred tax balances on the regulatory 
4 
accounts included in rate base were adjusted by any forecast movement in 
5 
those accounts in the Rate Year and Data Years. 
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Did the Company make any one-time adjustments to rate base ADIT? 
8 
A. 
The Company made two adjustments related to bonus depreciation that were 
9 
estimated to occur in December 2015. The first adjustment was an estimate 
10 
for bonus depreciation for January 2015 through March 2015 and was an 
11 
increase to ADIT of $9 million.  The second adjustment was an estimate of 
12 
bonus depreciation from April 2015 through September 2015 and was an 
13 
increase to ADIT of $14 million.  The one-time adjustments are to include the 
14 
impact of the PATH Act enacted in December 2015.  The Company estimated 
15 
these amounts by comparing plant additions after deducting repairs and 
16 
applying the 50 percent bonus deduction.  These adjustments only affect 
17 
federal ADIT because New York has decoupled from the bonus depreciation 
18 
provisions.   
19 
 
20

--- Page 120 ---

The Company made an additional adjustment in December 2015 to true-up 
1 
plant estimates to the actual March 2015 federal and state returns. The impact 
2 
to ADIT is an increase of $2.4 million.

C. 
Working Capital 
5 
Q. 
Has the Company recognized a working capital allowance associated with 
6 
gas purchases? 
7 
A. 
Yes.  Schedule 5 of Exhibit ___ (RRP-7) presents the calculation of the 
8 
carrying charge applicable to the working capital requirements associated with 
9 
gas purchases based on the lead-lag studies contained in this schedule.  The 
10 
Company used the same methodology as was approved by the Commission in 
11 
the 2012 Niagara Mohawk gas rate case for the gas study.    
12 
 
13 
Q. 
What are the lead-lag studies used to measure? 
14 
A. 
The lead-lag studies are used to measure the working capital needed by the 
15 
Company to support its gas purchases.  The Company is required to provide 
16 
working capital for the time between when the Company pays its suppliers for 
17 
gas purchases and the time the Company receives payments for these 
18 
purchases from its customers.  The results of the studies are used to determine 
19 
the working capital necessary for purchased gas expense.

XI. 
Inflation Factors 
13 
Q. 
Please explain Exhibit ___ (RRP-8). 
14 
A. 
Exhibit ___ (RRP-8) sets forth the table of inflation factors used to escalate 
15 
expense and capital expenditures from the Historic Test Year to the Rate Year 
16 
and Data Years.  The exhibit consists of a Summary Page detailing the 
17 
escalation rates.   
18 
 
19 
Q.  
What inflation adjustment did the Company use for O&M expense? 
20 
122


--- Page 125 ---
Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel  
Page 120 of 136 
A.  
The inflation adjustment factor for determining the level of O&M expense in 
1 
the Rate Year is 4.2964 percent.  This is equivalent to a 1.8872 percent 
2 
average annual increase between the fifteen months ended September 30, 
3 
2015 and the Rate Year. 
4 
 
5 
Q.  
How was this inflation adjustment factor calculated? 
6 
A.  
The calculation is detailed in Exhibit __ (RRP-8).  The approach uses the 
7 
GDP Chained Price Index (“GDPIPD”) as the measure of the price level and 
8 
percent changes in this index as the measure of inflation. The GDPIPD is 
9 
released quarterly by the US Department of Commerce, Bureau of Economic 
10 
Analysis.  The inflation adjustment factor is calculated by relating the average 
11 
of the GDPIPD in the Historic Test Year (the four quarters ended September 
12 
30, 2015) to forecast GDPIPD in the Rate Year (the four quarters ended 
13 
December 31, 2017).  Inflation between the Historic Test Year and Data Years 
14 
was calculated in a similar fashion   
15

Q.

20

21 
128

Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel

A. 
Currently, the Company defers for future recovery one hundred percent of 
1 
actual SIR costs, with the exception of the Coney Island and Citizens sites, 
2 
which are reconciled on a 90/10 percent basis.  As discussed above and in 
3

4 
current SIR deferral mechanism such that any difference between the actual 
5 
SIR expense (inclusive of Gowanus Canal and Newtown Creek) and the 
6 
level reflected in rates is recovered from or credited to customers through the 
7 
SIR Recovery Surcharge. The Company is not proposing a change to the 
8 
90/10 reconciliation mechanism currently in effect for the Coney Island and 
9 
Citizens sites.   
10 
 
11 
KEDNY is not proposing to change any other terms of the current SIR 
12 
deferral mechanism.  As such KEDNY will continue to: 
13 
 credit 100 percent of net recoveries from insurance carriers and/or 
14 
other potentially responsible parties to the deferral balance; and 
15 
 credit after-tax net gains from the sale of any property upon which 
16 
investigation and remediation activities have occurred to the total SIR 
17 
cost for the specific site.

transfers, National Grid was required under applicable accounting rules to

3

4

6 
Q. 
What is a curtailment? 
7 
A. 
Under US GAAP accounting rules, a curtailment is defined as an event that 
8 
significantly reduces the expected years of service to present employees or 
9 
eliminates for a significant number of employees the accrual of deferred 
10 
benefits for some or all of their future services. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
What is a settlement? 
13 
A. 
Under US GAAP accounting rules, a settlement is defined as an irrevocable 
14 
action that relieves the employer (or plan) of primary responsibility for a 
15 
benefit obligation and eliminates significant risks related to the obligation and 
16 
the assets used to effect the settlement. 
17 
 
18 
Q. 
What is prior service cost? 
19 
A. 
Prior service cost represents the cost of retroactive benefits granted in a plan 
20 
amendment. A plan amendment can increase or reduce the benefit obligation.  
21

Q. 
What is AOCI? 
11 
A. 
AOCI is a general ledger account in the equity section of a balance sheet.  It is 
12 
used to account for unrealized gains and losses such as pension plan gains or 
13 
losses.

Q. 
Why are pension and OPEB assets and liabilities exempt from carrying 
7 
charges? 
8 
A. 
Pursuant to the Commission’s P&OPEB Policy, utilities need only fund the 
9 
respective trusts when the amounts deferred are collected from customers.  
10 
Because the Company does not advance cash to fund the deferred amount, no 
11 
carrying charge is warranted.  When the Company recovers the deferral, it 
12 
places the funds into the pension or OPEB trusts.

requirement effect associated with the difference between the actual City/State

Appendix 2.

the full revenue requirement associated with any shortfall between actual

2013 – 2016 Deferrals 
 
In 2013, the City/State Construction deferral mechanism was eliminated and KEDNY’s 
capital tracker mechanism was modified to a downward-only net utility plant reconciliation 
mechanism that includes allocated common plant and depreciation expense.2  To the extent 
KEDNY’s net utility plant, inclusive of allocated common plant and depreciation expense, falls 
below the combined target average net utility plant and depreciation expense revenue 
requirement, KEDNY establishes a regulatory liability for the benefit of customers.  The 
mechanism was originally established to operate on a cumulative basis over the two-year term 
ending December 31, 2014, but was recently extended to four years by the Commission.3  In 
October 2015, the Commission modified KEDNY’s capital investment plan for 2015 and 2016, 
allowing KEDNY to use its deferred credit balance as of December 31, 2014 to offset the 
revenue requirement associated with its increased capital expenditures in 2015 and 2016.4

2. Coney Island Property Sale 
Basis for the Deferral 
 
On May 20, 2013, the Commission approved KEDNY’s sale of a 2.98 acre parcel of 
commercial property located at 809-873 Neptune Ave., Brooklyn, NY (“Coney Island” 
property).5  The Commission ordered KEDNY to record the $11.9 million net gain on the sale of 
the property as a credit to Account 254 – Other Regulatory Liabilities to be retained for the 
future benefit of customers and as a debit to Account 131.  The debit to Account 131 is necessary 
to recognize that the cash proceeds represent interest-free funds made available to the Company.  
To compensate customers, a carrying charge is accrued on the regulatory liability balance less 
the associated deferred income taxes using the Company’s Allowance for Funds Used During 
Construction rate.

4. 
Delivery Rate Adjustment 
 
Basis for the Deferral 
 
Pursuant to the December 2007 Order, KEDNY revised its tariff to incorporate a 
Delivery Rate Surcharge (“DRS”).6  Changes were made to the Customer Billing Charge to 
include the DRS, which went into effect on a temporary basis on January 1, 2008.  KEDNY’s 
tariff revisions to memorialize the DRS were permanently approved by the Commission in May 
2008.7  The revisions included the establishment of the Delivery Rate Adjustment (“DRA”) as a 
separate line item on customer bills.  The DRA consists of the DRS, a Weather Normalization 
Adjustment for applicable heating customers, the incremental State Assessment Charge and, 
where applicable, the Revenue Decoupling Mechanism.  The DRS provided for $5 million in 
annual rate increases beginning in Rate Year One and was ultimately designed to collect $25 
million by Rate Year Five.  The DRS expired at the end of 2012.

December 21, 2007) (hereinafter the “December 2007 Order”), at 67. 
7 Case 06-G-1185, Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, Charges, Rules and Regulations of The

6. Environmental Costs 
 
Basis for the Deferral 
 
Pursuant to JP-2, KEDNY defers for future recovery one hundred percent of actual SIR 
costs, with the exception of the Coney Island and Citizens sites, as discussed below.  Deferred 
SIR costs include the remediation costs associated with KEDNY’s manufactured gas plant sites 
and Superfund sites, including sites where KEDNY is named as a potentially responsible party.  
KEDNY generally recognizes its SIR responsibilities as a liability on its balance sheet as the 
sites are identified and costs are assessed. The liabilities are reduced as expenditures are made 
to clean up or remediate sites. The SIR deferral follows the cash expenditures.   
As described in Gas Rates Joint Proposal in Case 06-G-1185, if the total cost of 
investigating and remediating the Coney Island site, plus the cost for investigating the 
Company’s Citizens site, differs from $33.996 million, KEDNY would absorb or retain 10 
percent of the difference, and the balance would be reflected in the Balancing Account as either 
a debit or credit.11

the same.16 
 
Actual Deferral through September 30, 2015 
 
The actual deferral balance as of September 30, 2015 was a credit of $8.954 million. 
 
Forecast of Deferrals through December 31, 2016 
 
As of December 31, 2016, the forecast deferral balance is a credit of $8.954 million. 
 
10. Pension and Other Post Employment Benefits 
 
Basis for the Deferral 
 
Beginning Rate Year One (2008), KEDNY returned to the Commission’s Statement of 
Policy for Pensions and OPEBs.  KEDNY reconciles its actual pension and OPEB expense to the 
Estimated Pension and OPEB Expense amounts set forth in JP 2 Section IX(C)(1).  Fair value 
adjustments to the pension and OPEB plans made in connection with the National Grid merger 
are amortized over ten years in accordance with the Statement of Policy and reflected in the 
reconciliation of actual pension and OPEB expense to the Estimated Pension and OPEB expense 
in JP 2.  The reconciliation does not include settlement and curtailment costs from merger-
related programs as those costs are included in the costs to achieve.  Subsequent stay out years 
are reconciled to the Rate Year Five Pension and OPEB Expense allowances set forth in JP 2.17   
 
KEDNY also reduced its annual revenue requirement for the five rate years by $6 million 
as payment for returning to the pension policy.  The $30 million reduction, in addition to the $99 
million reduction in revenue requirements over the five years, was recorded as a regulatory 
liability and amortized over the five rate years to match the adjustments to the cost of service in 
                                                 
16 June 2013 Order, at 23-25. 
17 See generally September 2007 Order. 
148


--- Page 152 ---
Appendix A 
Testimony of KEDNY Revenue Requirements Panel 
Page 10 of 16 
 
 
JP 2, Appendix 2 and fund KEDNY’s Pension/OPEB plans in Rate Year One by $60 million, 
less the funding made by KEDNY since March 31, 2007.18 
Actual Deferral through September 30, 2015 
 
The actual deferral balance as of September 30, 2015 was $69.592 million. 
 
Forecast of Deferrals through December 31, 2016 
 
As of December 31, 2016, the forecast deferral balance is $82.378 million. 
 
11. Property Tax 
 
Basis for the Deferral 
 
KEDNY’s delivery rates during the rate period reflect an amortization over a seven year 
period of the deferred special franchise taxes it had incurred as of March 31, 2007, less a $22.5 
million write off.19  For the period from April 1, 2007 through September 30, 2007, KEDNY was 
permitted to defer 90 percent of the difference between its actual annualized special franchise tax 
and the annualized amount of $45.718 million established in Case 97-M-0567.20 
 
Actual Deferral through September 30, 2015 
 
The actual deferral balance as of September 30, 2015 was a credit of $5.184 million 
 
Forecast of Deferrals through December 31, 2016 
 
As of December 31, 2016, the forecast deferral balance is a credit of $9.809 million. 
 
12. Property Taxes After 2007 
 
Basis for the Deferral 
                                                 
18 JP 2, Section IX(C)(1) and September 2007 Order. 
19 JP 4, at 19; December 2007 Order, at 54. 
20 December 2007 Order, at 52, 63.

In accordance with the Order in Case 06-G-1185, KEDNY deferred 90 percent of the 
difference between its actual property and special franchise taxes and the amounts set forth in JP 
2 Section IX(C)(2) in Rate Years One through Five.  The other 10 percent of the difference was 
allocated to KEDNY.  Subsequent rate years continued the 90/10 allocation and are reconciled 
for property and special franchise taxes to the Rate Year Five allowance.   
 
Actual Deferral through September 30, 2015 
 
The actual deferral balance as of September 30, 2015 was $6.495 million. 
 
Forecast of Deferrals through December 31, 2016 
 
As of December 31, 2016, the forecast deferral balance is a credit of $7.776 million. 
 
13. Rate Mitigation 
 
Basis for the Deferral 
 
In Case 06-G-1185, the Commission adopted KEDNY’s five-year revenue increase 
settlement.  As part of the settlement, the Commission ordered that KENDY’s revenues were to 
be offset by the rate mitigators set forth in JP 2, Appendix 2, Page 8.21  Specifically, the 
Commission’s mitigators deferred recovery of certain deferred costs, reflected net synergy 
savings resulting from the National Grid merger, and modified KEDNY’s overall allowed rate of 
return.  Additionally, certain mitigating adjustments were required to be deferred in new 
regulatory liability accounts and amortized in revenues over the five-year period.  The additional 
mitigating adjustments included the costs associated with Pensions and OPEBs and additional 
margin adjustments.   
 
Actual Deferral through September 30, 2015 
 
The actual deferral balance as of September 30, 2015 was $7.805 million.

Forecast of Deferrals through December 31, 2016

14. Rate Subject to Refund 
 
Basis for the Deferral 
 
On May 22, 2014, the Company entered into a Joint Proposal with Staff in Cases 10-E-
0050, 10-M-0451 and 13-M-0026 in settlement of the affiliate cost allocation audit conducted by 
Overland Consulting.  The Joint Proposal, which was adopted by the Commission,22 established 
deferred credits for the benefit of customers in the amount of $13.336 million to provide 
adjustment for rates charged between 2008 and 2010.  The credits were recorded on the 
Company’s books as of May 22, 2014 and immediately began to accrue carrying charges (net of 
any federal and state income tax effects) at the KEDNY pre-tax rate of return.   
Actual Deferral through September 30, 2015 
The actual deferral balance as of September 30, 2015 was a credit of $14.449 million. 
 
Forecast of Deferrals through December 31, 2016 
 
As of December 31, 2016, the forecast deferral balance is a credit of $14.449 million. 
 
 
15. Regulatory Assessments  
 
Basis for the Deferral 
 
In 2009, New York State enacted a budget requiring the Department of Public Service to 
collect a Temporary State Energy and Utility Service Conservation Assessment (the “PSL 18-a 
Assessment”).  The Commission directed utilities to implement tariff revisions to collect the PSL

tariff. 
 
Actual Deferral through September 30, 2015 
The actual deferral balance as of September 30, 2015 was a credit of $3.209 million. 
 
Forecast of Deferrals through December 31, 2016

16. Transition Balancing Account 
 
Basis for the Deferral 
 
Deferral amounts arising from various mechanisms, including but not limited to Excess 
Earnings, Merchant Supplier Backout Credits, Consumer Outreach and Education, Interest on 
Deferred Items, Marketers Incentive, etc., are accumulated in the Transition Balancing Account.    
 
Actual Deferral through September 30, 2015 
 
The actual deferral balance as of September 30, 2015 was a credit of $71.163 
million. 
 
Forecast of Deferrals through December 31, 2016 
 
As of December 31, 2016, the forecast deferral balance is a credit of $71.163 million. 
 
17. Temperature Controlled and Interruptive Service Sharing 
 
Basis for the Deferral 
                                                 
23 Case 09-M-0311, Implementation of Chapter 59 of the Laws of 2009 Establishing a Temporary Annual 
Assessment Pursuant to Public Service Law § 18-a(6), Order Implementing Temporary State Assessment

The Joint Proposal dated February 22, 2013 in Case 12-G-0544 provides that KEDNY’s 
revenue requirements includes Temperature Controlled (“TC”) revenues of $100 million in Rate 
Year Six and $102.3 million in Rate Year Seven.  To the extent KEDNY’s actual TC revenues 
exceed those amounts, 90 percent of such excess revenues will be deferred for the benefit of 
customers and 10 percent will be retained by KEDNY.  To the extent the Company’s TC 
revenues are less than these levels, 90 percent of the TC revenue shortfall will be deferred for 
future recovery from customers. 
 
Actual Deferral through September 30, 2015 
 
The actual deferral balance as of September 30, 2015 was $54.419 million. 
 
Forecast of Deferrals through December 31, 2016 
 
As of December 31, 2016, the forecast deferral balance is $54.419 million.
